In addition to the calcium indicator (GCa\\r\\nMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labellin\\r\\ng strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p\\u2019s initial cross-session registration and tra\\r\\ncking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the \\u2018Benchmarking on manually tracked cells\\u2019 section). Before running Track2p, eac\\r\\nh recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suit\\r\\ne2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex developme\\r\\nnt. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for sum\\r\\nmary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (\\u00b1 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, c\\r\\norresponding to 33 % (\\u00b1 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a \\u2018motion energy\\u2019 metric.\\r\\n\\r\\nChronic 2-photon calcium imaging\\r\\n\\r\\nLongitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bru\\r\\nker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted ligh\\r\\nt was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the\\r\\nGCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-20\\r\\n0 \\u00b5m from the pial surface) with a 720 \\u00d7 720 \\u00b5m field of view and 512 \\u00d7 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted\\r\\n20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmi\\r\\nll (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessi\\r\\nons for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the\\r\\n red channel (tdTomato) from all previous recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging s\\r\\netup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates.\\r\\n\\r\\nVideography\\r\\n\\r\\nAll videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were re\\r\\ncorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modal\\r\\nities.\\r\\n\\r\\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\\r\\n\\r\\nCalcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvol\\r\\nution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI\\r\\n as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (usin\\r\\ng the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader\\r\\n that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_a\\r\\nnd_parameters).\\r\\nPreprocessing videography\\r\\n\\r\\nWe used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62 we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of con\\r\\nsecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all indivi\\r\\ndual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subs\\r\\nequent analyses.\\r\\n\\r\\nFunctional properties of tracked neurons\\r\\nCalcium event rates\\r\\n\\r\\nTo quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy\\u2019s peak detection algorithm (Python). For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging usi\\r\\nng a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate a\\r\\ns the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient a\\r\\ncross all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse.\\r\\n\\r\\nDecoding\\r\\n\\r\\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regress\\r\\nion optimises the weights (\\u03b2) that minimise the following loss function:\\r\\n\\r\\nL(\\\\beta) = \\\\|y - X\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2 + \\\\lambda\\\\|\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2\\r\\n\\r\\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and \\u03bb is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal \\u03bb and to reliably estimate the model perfor\\r\\nmance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65,66), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and te\\r\\nst data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal \\u03bb is performed by using the train set to fit models with different \\u03bb values and choosing the value corresponding\\r\\n to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in\\r\\n model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all poss\\r\\nible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the\\r\\n inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day decoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for\\r\\nthat given day and evaluated it on all other days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins\\r\\nof 10 consecutive timestamps. print('---CELL---')\\r\\n>     print(''.join(c['source']))\\r\\n> EOF\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# Analysing output of t2p\\r\\nThis is a short demo explaining how to explore the output of track2p and use the matched neurons/traces for custom downstream analysis.\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\nThe example here is for a 1 plane recording with simultaneous videography (given dataset is jm032).\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# imports\\r\\nimport os\\r\\nfrom types import SimpleNamespace\\r\\n\\r\\nimport numpy as np\\r\\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\\r\\n\\r\\nfrom scipy.stats import zscore\\r\\n\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n### Step by step guide (more detailed explanations below):\\r\\n\\r\\nEach point from this list matches one section of this notebook\\r\\n\\r\\n1) Load the output of track2p\\r\\n2) Find cells that are present in all recordings ('matched cells')\\r\\n3) Load the data from one example dataset and visualise it\\r\\n4) Load the activity of the matched cells\\r\\n5) Visualise the activity of matched cells\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n### 1) Load the output of track2p\\r\\nWe will load the `.npy` files: `t2p_output_path/track2p/plane#_match_mat.npy` and `t2p_output_path/track2p/track_ops.npy`. These are the matrix of cell matches\\r\\nfor all days and the settings respectively. For more info see the repo readme and documentation.\\r\\n\\r\\nNote: In this demo a single-plane recording is used, but it can be modified easily for multiplane compatility (just repeat the same procedure while looping thro\\r\\nugh planes)\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# this is the directory that contains a /track2p folder that is output by running the track2p algorithm\\r\\nt2p_save_path = '/Users/manonmantez/Desktop/el' # (change this based on your data)\\r\\nplane = 'plane0' # which plane to process (the example dataset is single-plane)\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# np.load() the match matrix (plane0_match_mat.npy)\\r\\nt2p_match_mat = np.load(os.path.join(t2p_save_path, 'track2p', f'{plane}_match_mat.npy'), allow_pickle=True)\\r\\n\\r\\n# np.load() settings (this contains suite2p paths etc.) (track_ops.npy)\\r\\ntrack_ops_dict = np.load(os.path.join(t2p_save_path, 'track2p', 'track_ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\\r\\ntrack_ops = SimpleNamespace(**track_ops_dict) # create dummy object from the track_ops dictionary\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n### 2) Find cells that are present in all recordings ('matched cells')\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\nNow from this matrix get the matches that are present on all days:\\r\\n\\r\\n- A matrix (`plane#_match_mat.npy`) containing the indices of matched neurons across the session for a given plane (`#` is the index of the plane). Since matchi\\r\\nng is done from first day to last, some neurons will not be sucessfully tracked after one or a few days. In this case the matrix contains `None` values. To get\\r\\nneurons tracked across all days only take the rows of the matrices containing no `None` values.\\r\\n\\r\\nNote: of course we can use cells that are not present on all days, but for now this is the intended use case for downstream analysis.\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# get the rows that do not contain any Nones (if track2p doesnt find a match for a cell across two consecutive days it will append a None) -> cells with no None\\r\\ns are cells matched across all days\\r\\nt2p_match_mat_allday = t2p_match_mat[~np.any(t2p_match_mat==None, axis=1), :]\\r\\n\\r\\nprint(f'Shape of match matrix for cells present on all days: {t2p_match_mat_allday.shape} (cells, days)')\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n### 3) Load the data from one example dataset and visualise it\\r\\n\\r\\nNote: The track_ops.npy ('settings file') contains all the paths to suite2p folders used when running track2p (see cell below)\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\nprint('Datasets used for t2p:\\\\n')\\r\\nfor ds_path in track_ops.all_ds_path:\\r\\n    print(ds_path)\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\nNow just to test if the paths work we can try to look at data of one of the recordings (in the case below we use the last one). For this part it is important to\\r\\n know a bit about how the suite2p structures the outputs: https://suite2p.readthedocs.io/en/latest/outputs.html (the important things will be the `ops.npy`, `st\\r\\nat.npy`, `iscell.npy` and the `F.npy`). There are also separate tutorials and demos for this so we won't go into so much detail.\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# lets take the last dataset\\r\\nlast_ds_path = track_ops.all_ds_path[-1]\\r\\nprint(f'We will look at the dataset saved at: {last_ds_path}')\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# load the three files\\r\\nlast_ops = np.load(os.path.join(last_ds_path, 'suite2p', plane, 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\\r\\nlast_f = np.load(os.path.join(last_ds_path, 'suite2p', plane, 'F.npy'), allow_pickle=True)\\r\\niscell = np.load(os.path.join(last_ds_path, 'suite2p', plane, 'iscell.npy'), allow_pickle=True)\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# we filter the traces based on suite2p's iscell probability (note: it is crucial to use the same probability as in the track2p settings to keep the correct ind\\r\\nexing of matches)\\r\\niscell_thr = track_ops.iscell_thr\\r\\n\\r\\nprint(f'The iscell threshold used when running track2p was: {iscell_thr}')\\r\\n\\r\\nif track_ops\\n[... output limited to 10000 bytes; 68 interior bytes omitted ...]\\nst_f_iscell = last_f[iscell[:, 0] == 1, :]\\r\\n\\r\\nelse:\\r\\n    last_f_iscell = last_f[iscell[:, 1] > iscell_thr, :]\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# now first plot the mean image of the movie (it is saved in ops.npy, for more info see the suite2p outputs documentation)\\r\\nplt.imshow(last_ops['meanImg'], cmap='gray')\\r\\nplt.axis('off')\\r\\nplt.title('Mean image')\\r\\nplt.show()\\r\\n\\r\\nplt.figure(figsize=(10, 1))\\r\\nnonmatch_nrn_idx = 0\\r\\nplt.plot(last_f[nonmatch_nrn_idx, :])\\r\\nplt.xlabel('Frame')\\r\\nplt.ylabel('F')\\r\\nplt.title(f'Example trace (nrn_idx: {nonmatch_nrn_idx})')\\r\\nplt.show()\\r\\n\\r\\nplt.figure(figsize=(10, 3))\\r\\nplt.imshow(zscore(last_f_iscell, axis=1), aspect='auto', cmap='Greys', vmin=0, vmax=1.96)\\r\\nplt.xlabel('Frame')\\r\\nplt.ylabel('ROI')\\r\\nplt.title('Raster plot')\\r\\nplt.show()\\r\\n\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n## 4) Load the activity of the matched cells\\r\\n\\r\\nNow that we know how to look at data in one recording we will use the output from track2p to look at activity of the same cells across all datasets.\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\nTo do this we need to loop through all datasets and:\\r\\n- load the files described above\\r\\n- filter `stat.npy` and `fluo.npy` by the track2p iscell threshold (classical suite2p)\\r\\n- filter `stat.npy` and `fluo.npy` by the appropriate indices from the matrix of neurons matched on all days (additional filtering step after track2p)\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will produce a nice data structure where the indices of cells are matched within the stat and fluo objects. Sorting the object in this way allows for very\\r\\nstraightforward extraction of matched data (see cells below)\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\niscell_thr = track_ops.iscell_thr # use the same threshold as when running the algo (to be consistent with indexing)\\r\\n\\r\\nall_stat_t2p = []\\r\\nall_f_t2p = []\\r\\nall_ops = [] # ops dont change\\r\\n\\r\\nfor (i, ds_path) in enumerate(track_ops.all_ds_path):\\r\\n    ops = np.load(os.path.join(ds_path, 'suite2p', plane, 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\\r\\n    stat = np.load(os.path.join(ds_path, 'suite2p', plane, 'stat.npy'), allow_pickle=True)\\r\\n    f = np.load(os.path.join(ds_path, 'suite2p', plane, 'F.npy'), allow_pickle=True)\\r\\n    iscell = np.load(os.path.join(ds_path, 'suite2p', plane, 'iscell.npy'), allow_pickle=True)\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n    if track_ops.iscell_thr==None:\\r\\n        stat_iscell = stat[iscell[:, 0] == 1]\\r\\n        f_iscell = f[iscell[:, 0] == 1, :]\\r\\n\\r\\n    else:\\r\\n        stat_iscell = stat[iscell[:, 1] > iscell_thr]\\r\\n        f_iscell = f[iscell[:, 1] > iscell_thr, :]\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n    stat_t2p = stat_iscell[t2p_match_mat_allday[:,i].astype(int)]\\r\\n    f_t2p = f_iscell[t2p_match_mat_allday[:,i].astype(int), :]\\r\\n\\r\\n    all_stat_t2p.append(stat_t2p)\\r\\n    all_f_t2p.append(f_t2p)\\r\\n    all_ops.append(ops)\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n### 5) Visualise the ROIs and the activity of (a) matched cell(s)\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nThis example shows how to extract the information of a ROI from all_stat. We first index by the day to get stat_t2p from all_stat2p (this is the sorted stat obj\\r\\nect for that day). We can then get the roi information by indexing stat_t2p by the index of the cell match (because of resorting we use the same index across da\\r\\nys).\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\nwind = 24\\r\\nnrn_idx = 0\\r\\n\\r\\nfor i in range(len(track_ops.all_ds_path)):\\r\\n    mean_img = all_ops[i]['meanImg']\\r\\n    stat_t2p = all_stat_t2p[i]\\r\\n    median_coord = stat_t2p[nrn_idx]['med']\\r\\n\\r\\n    plt.figure(figsize=(1.5,1.5))\\r\\n    plt.imshow(mean_img[int(median_coord[0])-wind:int(median_coord[0])+wind, int(median_coord[1])-wind:int(median_coord[1])+wind], cmap='gray') # plot a short w\\r\\nindow around the ROI centroid\\r\\n    plt.scatter(wind, wind)\\r\\n    plt.axis('off')\\r\\n    plt.show()\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n# first plot the trace of cell c for all days\\r\\nnrn_idx = 0 # the activity of the ROI visualised above on all days\\r\\n\\r\\nfor i in range(len(track_ops.all_ds_path)):\\r\\n    plt.figure(figsize=(10, 1)) # make a wide figure\\r\\n    plt.plot(all_f_t2p[i][nrn_idx, :])\\r\\n    plt.xlabel('Frame')\\r\\n    plt.ylabel('F')\\r\\n    plt.show()\\r\\n\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\nNow to visualise the rasters its a simple exercise, since they are already sorted in a way that the rows represent the same cell across days we don't need to do\\r\\n anything other than simply looping through all_f_t2p and plotting each element as we did before.\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\nfor i in range(len(track_ops.all_ds_path)):\\r\\n    plt.figure(figsize=(10, 3)) # make a wide figure\\r\\n    f_plot = zscore(all_f_t2p[i], axis=1)\\r\\n    plt.imshow(f_plot, aspect='auto', cmap='Greys', vmin=0, vmax=1.96)\\r\\n    plt.xlabel('Frame')\\r\\n---CELL---\\r\\n### The End!\\r\\n\\r\\nCongrats! This period\\r\\nmarks a transition from immature, fragmented states to more organized and regulated sleep-\\r\\nwake patterns, along with increasing influence from neuromodulatory and sensory systems.\\r\\nAll of these changes have been recently reviewed in Wu et al. 2024 (see also Martini et al.\\r\\n2021). In addition, in the developing somatosensory system, before postnatal day 11 (P11),\\r\\nwake-related movements (reafference) are actively gated and blocked by the external\\r\\ncuneate nucleus (ECN, Tiriac et al. 2016 and all excellent recent work from the Blumberg lab).\\r\\nThis gating prevents sensory feedback from wake movements from reaching the cortex,\\r\\nensuring that only sleep-related twitches drive neural responses. However, around P11, this\\r\\ngating mechanism abruptly lifts, enabling sensory signals from wake movements to influence\\r\\ncortical processing\\u2014signaling a dramatic developmental shift from Wu et al. 2024\\r\\nReviewer #1 (Recommendations for the authors):\\r\\nThis manuscript represents a significant advancement in the field of developmental\\r\\nneuroscience, offering a powerful and elegant framework for longitudinal cellular\\r\\ntracking using the Track2p method combined with robust analytical approaches. Neuropil correction: Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu\\n    2. Baseline estimation using maximin method\\n    3. dF/F = (Fc - baseline) / baseline\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    # Neuropil correction\\n    Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu\\n    \\n    # Baseline estimation (maximin method)\\n    win = int(win_baseline * fs)  # dF/F\\n    dff = (Fc - Flow) / np.maximum(Flow, 1e-6)\\n    \\n    return dff.astype(np.float32)\\n\\n\\ndef bin_data(data, bin_size, axis=-1):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Average data in non-overlapping bins along specified axis.\\n    \\n    Truncates to largest multiple of bin_size.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    n = data.shape[axis]\\n    n_bins = n // bin_size\\n    n_use = n_bins * bin_size\\n    \\n    if axis == -1 or axis == len(data.shape) - 1:\\n        data_trunc = data[..., :n_use]\\n        new_shape = data.shape[:-1] + (n_bins, bin_size)\\n        return data_trunc.reshape(new_shape).mean(axis=-1)\\n    elif axis == 0:\\n        data_trunc = data[:n_use]\\n        new_shape = (n_bins, bin_size) + data.shape[1:]\\n        return data_trunc.reshape(new_shape).mean(axis=1)\\n    else:\\n        raise ValueError(f\\\"Unsupported axis {axis}\\\")\\n\\n\\ndef align_motion_energy(me, n_neural_frames):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Align motion energy to neural frames.\\n    \\n    Motion energy may have fewer frames due to missing camera frames.\\n    Pad with NaN or interpolate to match neural frame count.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    if len(me) == n_neural_frames:\\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\\n    elif len(me) < n_neural_frames:\\n        # Interpolate missing frames\\n        me_aligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\\n        me_aligned[:len(me)] = me.astype(np.float64)\\n        # Simple linear interpolation for missing end frames\\n        if np.any(np.isnan(me_aligned)):\\n            valid = ~np.isnan(me_aligned)\\n            indices = np.arange(n_neural_frames)\\n            me_aligned = np.interp(indices, indices[valid], me_aligned[valid])\\n        return me_aligned\\n    else:\\n        # Truncate if somehow longer\\n        return me[:n_neural_frames].astype(np.float64)\\n\\n\\ndef normalize_motion_energy(me):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Normalize motion energy to [0, 1] range.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    me_min = np.nanmin(me)\\n    me_max = np.nanmax(me)\\n    if me_max - me_min < 1e-10:\\n        return np.zeros_like(me)\\n    return (me - me_min) / (me_max - me_min)\\n\\n\\ndef discretize_to_bins(values, n_bins=5):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Discretize continuous values into equal-percentile bins.\\n    \\n    Returns integer bin labels 0..n_bins-1.\\n    Percentile edges are computed to create equal-frequency bins.\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\\n    edges = np.percentile(values, percentiles)\\n    # The key discrepancy identified: the Track2p GUI has `neucoeff=0.0` while the paper says \"Suite2p defaults,\" which are `neucoeff=0.7`.\n\n---\n\n## Motion Energy Processing\n\n### Raw Data\n- File: `motion_energy_glob.npy`, dtype `uint64`\n- Values: sum of squared pixel differences between consecutive video frames\n- First value is 0 (no previous frame)\n- Sometimes has slightly fewer frames than neural data (missing camera frames)\n\n### Alignment Handling\nThe AI implemented interpolation for missing frames:\n```python\ndef align_motion_energy(me, n_neural_frames):\n    if len(me) == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    elif len(me) < n_neural_frames:\n        me_aligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n        me_aligned[:len(me)] = me.astype(np.float64)\n        # Linear interpolation over missing values\n        valid = ~np.isnan(me_aligned)\n        me_aligned = np.interp(indices, indices[valid], me_aligned[valid])\n```\n\n### Processing Pipeline\n1. Align to neural frame count via interpolation\n2. Bin by averaging 10 consecutive frames (matching methods.txt)\n3. Normalize per-session: `(me - me_min) / (me_max - me_min)`\n4. Discretize into 5 equal-percentile bins using `np.digitize`\n\n### Timestamps\nThe AI discovered that `tstamps.npy` is in **kiloseconds** (total span ~1.21 for 36000 frames at 30 Hz = 1200 seconds = 1.2 ks). The `interframe_int.npy` gives ~33.6 μs intervals = 1/30 Hz in ks units.\n\n---\n\n## Issues and Bugs Found and Fixed\n\n### Bug 1: dF/F Division Error (Critical)\n- **Found**: Session neural data had values up to 10⁸ (mean=9085, std=574462 for session 1 vs. expected ~0.4, std ~0.58)\n- **Cause**: Division by near-zero/negative baseline `(Fc - Flow) / np.maximum(Flow, 1e-6)` — 7 neurons had `Flow < 0`\n- **Fix**: Changed to subtraction-only: `dff = Fc - Flow`, matching Suite2p source code\n- **Effect**: After fix, all sessions had reasonable values; training accuracy jumped from 0.27/0.18 to 0.60/0.32\n\n### Issue 2: neucoeff Discrepancy\n- **Found**: Track2p GUI `F_processing` uses `neucoeff=0.0` while paper says \"Suite2p defaults\"\n- **Resolution**: Used `neucoeff=0.7` from `ops.npy` (Suite2p defaults), per paper instruction\n- This was the correct interpretation\n\n---\n\n## Key Statistics Compared Against Paper\n\n| Statistic | Paper | Data | Converted |\n|-----------|-------|------|-----------|\n| Subjects | 6 | 6 | 6 ✓ |\n| Mean neurons/mouse | 526 ± 190 | 499.7 | 498.66 ✓ |\n| Sessions total | ≥6/mouse | 41 | 41 ✓ |\n| Frame rate | 30 Hz | 30 Hz | 30 Hz ✓ |\n| Session duration | 20 min | 20 min (jm031/32), 30 min (others) | Used all data |\n| Bin size | 10 frames | — | 10 frames ✓ |\n| iscell threshold | 0.5 | All = 1.0 (pre-filtered) | Pre-filtered ✓ |\n\nThe AI noted a discrepancy: the paper states \"20 minutes\" per session, but 4 of 6 mice (jm038, jm039, jm040, jm046) had 54,000 frames = 30 minutes. Temporal binning: average in non-overlapping bins of 10 frames\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, fs=30.0, neucoeff=0.7, sig_baseline=10.0, win_baseline=60.0):\n    Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu\n    win = int(win_baseline * fs)  # 1800 frames\n    Flow = gaussian_filter(Fc.astype(np.float64), [0., sig_baseline])\n    Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, win, axis=1)\n    Flow = maximum_filter1d(Flow, win, axis=1)\n    dff = Fc - Flow\n    return dff.astype(np.float32)\n\ndff_binned = bin_data(dff, BIN_SIZE, axis=-1)  # BIN_SIZE=10\n```\n\niii. The AI handles this by linear interpolation to match the neural frame count. No other missing data handling is implemented - the code assumes neural data is complete and well-formed. The `normalize_motion_energy` function has a guard for zero-range data (returns zeros).\n\nii.\n```python\n# Missing ME frames: interpolate\nme_aligned = align_motion_energy(me, n_frames)\n\n# Zero-range protection in normalization\ndef normalize_motion_energy(me):\n    me_min = np.nanmin(me)\n    me_max = np.nanmax(me)\n    if me_max - me_min < 1e-10:\n        return np.zeros_like(me)\n    return (me - me_min) / (me_max - me_min)\n```\n\niii. The `ops.npy` file provides parameters (frame rate, baseline correction settings).\n\nii.\n```python\nF = np.load(os.path.join(session_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0', 'F.npy'))\nFneu = np.load(os.path.join(session_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0', 'Fneu.npy'))\nops = np.load(os.path.join(session_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0', 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\n```\n\niii. The AI documented this in CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 1, noting these are standard Suite2p output files. The load_data.ipynb notebook also loads F.npy and suggests computing dF/F \"the way as described in the paper.\"\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The processing pipeline is:\n1. Neuropil correction: `Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu`\n2. Baseline estimation using maximin method (gaussian filter -> min filter -> max filter)\n3. Baseline subtraction: `dff = Fc - Flow` (subtraction only, no division)\n4. Temporal binning: average in non-overlapping bins of 10 frames\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, fs=30.0, neucoeff=0.7, sig_baseline=10.0, win_baseline=60.0):\n    Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu\n    win = int(win_baseline * fs)  # 1800 frames\n    Flow = gaussian_filter(Fc.astype(np.float64), [0., sig_baseline])\n    Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, win, axis=1)\n    Flow = maximum_filter1d(Flow, win, axis=1)\n    dff = Fc - Flow\n    return dff.astype(np.float32)\n\ndff_binned = bin_data(dff, BIN_SIZE, axis=-1)  # BIN_SIZE=10\n```\n\niii. The AI initially implemented dF/F as `(Fc - Flow) / Flow` (traditional definition) but discovered this caused extreme values. After inspecting the Suite2p source code (`dcnv.py`), the AI found that Suite2p's `baseline_maximin` performs subtraction only, not division. The AI corrected to `Fc - Flow`, documented this bug fix in CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7. The AI chose neucoeff=0.7 based on ops.npy and the paper stating \"default Suite2p parameters,\" despite the Track2p GUI code using neucoeff=0.0.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No neuron-level filtering is applied. The AI determined that all neurons in the provided data have `iscell[:,0] == 1.0`, meaning they were already pre-filtered by Track2p's cell detection and tracking pipeline.\n\nii. No filtering code exists in convert_data.py. The AI loads all neurons from F.npy without any exclusion.\n\niii. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to the start of the recording session. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The temporal resolution is 333.33 ms (10 frames at 30 Hz). Temporal rebinning is applied: the raw 30 Hz data is averaged in non-overlapping bins of 10 consecutive frames.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_SIZE = 10           # frames to average\nFRAME_RATE = 30         # Hz\nTIME_BIN_MS = 1000.0 * BIN_SIZE / FRAME_RATE  # 333.33 ms\n\ndef bin_data(data, bin_size, axis=-1):\n    n = data.shape[axis]\n    n_bins = n // bin_size\n    n_use = n_bins * bin_size\n    data_trunc = data[..., :n_use]\n    new_shape = data.shape[:-1] + (n_bins, bin_size)\n    return data_trunc.reshape(new_shape).mean(axis=-1)\n```\n\niii. From methods.txt: \"For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from start of experiment* derived from?\n\ni. The input is not derived from any raw data variable. It is computed synthetically from the bin indices, using the bin size and frame rate to convert bin index to elapsed time in seconds.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_bins = (np.arange(start, end) * TIME_BIN_MS / 1000.0).astype(np.float32)\ninput_trials.append(time_bins.reshape(1, -1))\n```\n\niii. The AI computed time elapsed from the start of the recording session based on the bin index. The time is continuous across trials within a session (trial 0 starts at 0s, trial 1 starts at 120s, etc.).\n\n## 3-b. No further processing. The time represents seconds elapsed from the start of the recording session.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_bins = (np.arange(start, end) * TIME_BIN_MS / 1000.0).astype(np.float32)\n# For trial 0: start=0, end=360 -> [0.0, 0.333, 0.667, ..., 119.67]\n# For trial 1: start=360, end=720 -> [120.0, 120.333, ..., 239.67]\n```\n\niii. How is `output` *Motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Motion energy is aligned to neural frames before any processing. If the motion energy has fewer frames than neural data (due to missing camera frames), the missing values at the end are filled by linear interpolation. If longer, it is truncated. Both neural and motion energy data are then binned identically (10-frame bins), ensuring temporal alignment.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef align_motion_energy(me, n_neural_frames):\n    if len(me) == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    elif len(me) < n_neural_frames:\n        me_aligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n        me_aligned[:len(me)] = me.astype(np.float64)\n        valid = ~np.isnan(me_aligned)\n        indices = np.arange(n_neural_frames)\n        me_aligned = np.interp(indices, indices[valid], me_aligned[valid])\n        return me_aligned\n    else:\n        return me[:n_neural_frames].astype(np.float64)\n```\n\niii. The data README notes: \"In some recordings there might be some missing frames from the camera\" and suggests the frames \"can be interpolated over.\" The AI's approach of placing available frames at the start and interpolating is a reasonable but imperfect solution - ideally, the specific missing frame indices from `tstamps.npy` or `interframe_int.npy` should be used to identify which frames are missing.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. The main data quality issue is missing motion energy frames (camera frames can be fewer than neural frames). The AI handles this by linear interpolation to match the neural frame count. No other missing data handling is implemented - the code assumes neural data is complete and well-formed. The `normalize_motion_energy` function has a guard for zero-range data (returns zeros).\n\nii.\n```python\n# Missing ME frames: interpolate\nme_aligned = align_motion_energy(me, n_frames)\n\n# Zero-range protection in normalization\ndef normalize_motion_energy(me):\n    me_min = np.nanmin(me)\n    me_max = np.nanmax(me)\n    if me_max - me_min < 1e-10:\n        return np.zeros_like(me)\n    return (me - me_min) / (me_max - me_min)\n```\n\niii. The AI noted the missing frames issue from the data README and chose to interpolate.
